Down 4 from 8 at w/e 2026-09-18 — the sixth consecutive decline (58 → 64 → 41 → 52 → 48 unpublished → 46 → 8 → 4). Last week the resignation was 12 hours and 46 minutes old and still salvageable. This week it is eight days old, ALTR's first response to it was a bill sent four days later, and the client has paid and gone. The window contained six messages: a payment chase, an @-mention to chase the chase, 'No worries', a receipt, 'Final invoice paid', and 'Thanks @alexpaigem'. That is the complete record of how ALTR handled the departure of a client who paid it for twelve months and never once withheld a cent. Not one person asked why she left. Derrek's silence reached 43 days and was never broken — personal owner contact was the number-one recommendation in five consecutive reports and it is now unactionable. Real credit where it is due: Stephski stated the balance clearly ('remaining balance is currently $5,259.90'), the third figure ever written in this chat, and collected it in 33 hours; the client paid it in full. But that is ALTR protecting its own receivable inside an otherwise total abandonment. Against it: no exit conversation, no reason captured, no save attempt, no offboarding or access handover, no confirmation the account is settled at zero, the $1,989 July dispute 60 days open and never mentioned in the closeout, ALTR's own referral debt to her 63 days unquantified, paid customs 31 days unrefunded on an account with $1,560 of chargeback history, and Michelle Queue's eight-week leak-report streak now two weeks broken. The score is not zero only because she paid, she left warmly, and she is still replying — which means the final ledger and the refunds can still be done today. It will be zero next week if they are not.

Suggestions

  • DERREK MESSAGES HER TODAY, PERSONALLY, AND NOT ABOUT MONEY. Eight days, and the only two things ALTR has said to a departing twelve-month client are 'shoot that over in the AM' and 'Thanks'. He has been silent on this account for 43 days. Say: thank you for twelve months, I'm sorry nobody asked how you were after your surgery, I'm sorry we never delivered the reels page, and if you'd give me fifteen minutes I'd like to understand what we got wrong. Retention is gone; reputation and referral are not, and she is still replying.
  • Run the exit conversation this week and write the answer down verbatim. Do not let this account close with the churn cause recorded only as a report's inference. If the answer turns out to be one of the four things already named in five consecutive reports, that finding — that the agency knew and did not act — is more valuable than the interview itself.
  • Send the final itemised statement within 24 hours and confirm the account is settled at zero. She has paid $5,259.90 without any way to verify it. Include every invoice total and payment amount from Jul 1, the $1,989 July dispute, the $4,160 and $2,434 offsets, and the referral money ALTR owes HER. If the net position favours her, pay her. A departed creator who later works out she overpaid is a public-review problem, not a bookkeeping one.
  • Refund the customs from the Aug 25 spreadsheet now. 31 days, creator gone, fans stalled with her own recorded voice, and prior chargeback exposure of $1,560 in 24 hours. This is the largest quantifiable money risk left and it did not move in the same week ALTR successfully collected its own balance.
  • Offboard her properly: tell her whether DMCA/leak scanning is still running or has stopped, revoke ALTR's access to her OF and socials, remove her from management-run pages, and tell her to rotate the password she handed over in 2025. This is a client-safety obligation and it is seven days late.
  • Fix the sequencing rule that produced this week: never let a billing chase be the first thing an agency says to a client after they resign. Build a hard rule — a resignation freezes automated and routine billing contact until the owner has responded personally, and the closing invoice goes out with, not before, the acknowledgement.
  • Make the report-to-owner loop the post-mortem's actual subject. Personal owner contact on this account was the number-one recommendation in FIVE consecutive reports and was never actioned once. The churn was predicted in writing on 2026-09-11. The analysis worked; nothing downstream of it did. Fix what sits between this file and the owner's inbox before the next account repeats it.
  • Screen the roster this week for the exact churn signature: owner silent 30+ days, a disclosed personal or medical event never acknowledged, a long-promised deliverable with no proof, and a chat where most ALTR messages are billing. Alex Paige Moore matched all four for over a month before she left.
  • Adopt two one-line billing policies agency-wide: write the amount in-channel every single time (twelve months on this account produced three numbers), and set payout-aligned terms as the default for every creator rather than a 7-day window most of them structurally cannot meet.
  • Stop counting an account as 'handled' when the invoice clears. The close-out sequence here — chase, payment, 'Thanks' — is operationally complete and relationally catastrophic, and it will read identically in the next client's chat unless somebody owns the human half of an exit.

Down 30 from 35. The client terminated the engagement on 2026-09-21 and ALTR has not replied to the termination notice in the 3 days and 20 hours to the window close. There is no partial credit available here. A relationship that ends with the client offering to pay is one that can in principle be salvaged or at least offboarded without loss. Everything else is failure. The churn was signalled, in writing, in this report's own file, seven times: the growth-and-marketing complaint escalated on 2026-05-20, 2026-06-02, 2026-06-04, 2026-07-21, 2026-09-02, 2026-09-15 and finally 2026-09-21. Neither happened.
